Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Most nights I end up eating a lot of food when I’m not even hungry, almost like I’m on autopilot after the day is over. It’s become a regular pattern that I’m really struggling to manage on my own, which is why I decided to come in.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes of eating when you're not hungry, do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself or control what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. Once I start, it's like a switch flips and part of my brain is telling me to stop, but my hands just keep reaching for more food. I feel completely powerless to put the fork down in those moments.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any emotional distress, such as guilt or shame, after these episodes of eating when you're not hungry?\n\nPatient: Yes, I feel an overwhelming sense of shame and embarrassment afterward, which has made me avoid social plans because I just feel so worn out. It's actually why I wait until my daughter is in her room and my mom is settled for the night to eat, because I'm too embarrassed for anyone to see me doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest in sexual activities or a decrease in your sexual desire around the same time these eating episodes started?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any changes in my sexual desire, to be honest. With everything going on with my mom and work, it's just not something I've really thought about or experienced lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'with everything going on' and if you've experienced any significant stress or life changes around the time these eating episodes began?\n\nPatient: About ten months ago, my mother experienced several health setbacks, and I became her primary caregiver on top of supervising my administrative team at work. Juggling all of this caused me to drop my usual stress-relief habits like yoga and walks, and I started postponing meals until late in the evening. That's exactly when this overwhelming eating pattern really took hold.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you typically feel or what emotions you experience during the day, especially when dealing with your mother's health issues and work responsibilities, before these episodes of eating when you're not hungry occur?\n\nPatient: During the day, I feel incredibly stressed and overwhelmed, like my brain is in constant overdrive trying to manage my team at work and coordinate my mom's care. I'm naturally a very worry-prone person, so when I finally sit down at night with my planner, the anxiety about deadlines and appointments triggers that urge to start eating on autopilot.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if the feelings of stress, anxiety, and being overwhelmed that you experience during the day directly trigger the urge to eat when you're not hungry, or if there's a specific emotional shift or event that occurs right before these episodes?\n\nPatient: It is definitely tied to a specific shift in the evening. During the busy day, I'm just rushing around, but the moment I finally sit down at the kitchen table with my planner to organize my mom's appointments and work deadlines, all that worry and stress suddenly catches up with me. That transition is exactly what triggers me to start going back and forth to the fridge on autopilot.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any other situations or emotional states, aside from the evening planning and stress, that trigger these episodes of eating when you're not hungry?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any other specific triggers for this behavior. It almost always seems tied to that late-evening wind-down period when I'm sitting with my planner, worrying about my mom and work. That's really the only time it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are more about coping with the emotional stress and anxiety of the day, or is it primarily about the loss of control over eating itself that's distressing to you?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. It definitely starts as a way to cope and numb out the intense stress of my day, but the complete loss of control once I begin is what is truly distressing. Feeling so powerless to stop is deeply shameful for me, especially since I am normally such a structured and organized person."

I'm naturally a very worry-prone person, so when I finally sit down at night with my planner, the anxiety about deadlines and appointments triggers that urge to start eating on autopilot.\n\nDoctor: Can you clarify if the feelings of stress, anxiety, and being overwhelmed that you experience during the day directly trigger the urge to eat when you're not hungry, or if there's a specific emotional shift or event that occurs right before these episodes?\n\nPatient: It is definitely tied to a specific shift in the evening. During the busy day, I'm just rushing around, but the moment I finally sit down at the kitchen table with my planner to organize my mom's appointments and work deadlines, all that worry and stress suddenly catches up with me. That transition is exactly what triggers me to start going back and forth to the fridge on autopilot.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there are any other situations or emotional states, aside from the evening planning and stress, that trigger these episodes of eating when you're not hungry?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed any other specific triggers for this behavior. It almost always seems tied to that late-evening wind-down period when I'm sitting with my planner, worrying about my mom and work. That's really the only time it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are more about coping with the emotional stress and anxiety of the day, or is it primarily about the loss of control over eating itself that's distressing to you?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. It definitely starts as a way to cope and numb out the intense stress of my day, but the complete loss of control once I begin is what is truly distressing. Feeling so powerless to stop is deeply shameful for me, especially since I am normally such a structured and organized person.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you have experienced any other maladaptive coping mechanisms or behaviors besides this eating pattern, such as substance use or excessive spending, as a way to deal with your stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like substance use or excessive spending to cope with the stress. This eating pattern is really the main struggle I've been facing, and it feels like the only area where I've lost control."
  }
]

It almost always seems tied to that late-evening wind-down period when I'm sitting with my planner, worrying about my mom and work. That's really the only time it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are more about coping with the emotional stress and anxiety of the day, or is it primarily about the loss of control over eating itself that's distressing to you?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. It definitely starts as a way to cope and numb out the intense stress of my day, but the complete loss of control once I begin is what is truly distressing. Feeling so powerless to stop is deeply shameful for me, especially since I am normally such a structured and organized person.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you have experienced any other maladaptive coping mechanisms or behaviors besides this eating pattern, such as substance use or excessive spending, as a way to deal with your stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like substance use or excessive spending to cope with the stress. This eating pattern is really the main struggle I've been facing, and it feels like the only area where I've lost control.\n\nDoctor: Can you further describe what you mean by feeling 'powerless' during these episodes, and is this loss of control specific to eating or do you feel it in other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: When I say powerless, I mean it literally feels like a switch flips where my brain is screaming at me to stop, but my hands just keep reaching for food anyway. This loss of control is completely specific to eating; in every other area of my life, like managing my team at work, my mom's care, and our finances, I am incredibly organized and in charge. That is why this one area of complete powerlessness feels so jarring and out of character for me."
  }
]
